UPSC CSE Prelims · General Studies Paper I

Previous-year question analysis, 2009–2025

All 1,759 unique questions in the 2009–2025 compilation, read one by one and grouped into ten subjects. Each subject keeps the six parts of the UPPCS analysis in the same order, then adds eleven parts on how questions are built, how they trap, how they map onto UPPCS and what the common notes should contain.

How the counts were made

Source and coverage. The source is the Civilsdaily compilation “Microtheme Prelims [PYQs 2009–2025]”, which lists 1,760 GS-I questions under 11 compilation subjects and numbered microthemes. One question (S10.3, the 2018 PSLV/GSLV question) is printed twice, so there are 1,759 unique questions. The compilation holds every question from 2011 to 2025 (100 a year, 99 unique in 2018 after removing the duplicate) and 135 of 150 questions for 2009 and 125 of 150 for 2010. The 40 missing 2009–10 questions are not in the source and are not estimated; every total in this page reconciles to 1,759, and each year reconciles to the counts above.

Question IDs. Every question keeps the compilation’s own reference: subject letter, microtheme number and position (for example P2.6 = Polity, microtheme 2, question 6). The letters are P Polity, E Economy, G Geography, A Agriculture, M Modern History, V Environment, I International Relations, N Governance, C Art & Culture, S Science & Technology, X Miscellaneous.

Where a question is counted. Each question was tagged to exactly one of ten analysis subjects and one topic by what decides the answer, not by where the compilation filed it. A Governance-microtheme question on pension schemes is counted under Economy (schemes); a Geography-microtheme question on places in the news is counted under International Relations; a Polity-microtheme question on Marx is counted under Polity (political theory). Current-affairs questions sit under the static subject whose knowledge decides them; only sports, persons, books, awards, companies and defence organisation remain under Current Affairs & Miscellaneous.

Fact clusters. Inside each topic, questions testing the same body of fact were given a shared cluster label (for example “Article 21 expansion” or “money supply and multiplier”). The clusters are the sub-topics used in part 6, in the taxonomy and in the notes architecture.

Static vs current. Static (S): answerable from standard static knowledge. Current-linked static (CS): triggered by news, but the answer rests on static knowledge. Dynamic (D): the answer is the news fact itself. Hybrid (H): needs both a static concept and a current figure.

Cognitive demand. Recall (R): one standard fact decides it. Concept (C): an understanding of how something works decides it. Application (A): a concept must be applied to a new case or description. Peripheral (X): a fact outside standard coverage, which only wide reading answers.

Difficulty on the UPPCS scale. To compare the two exams directly, every question was scored with the UPPCS analysis’s own rules: format points (direct 0; two-statement, multiple-select and pair 1; three-plus statement, match, sequence and Statement I/II 2; “how many statements” 2, “how many pairs” and “how many items” 1), plus 1 for multiple-select or pair lists of three or more items, plus 1 for exact figures or dates in the items or options, plus 1 for negative framing in the question sentence, plus 1 for long descriptive options (55+ characters on average) in direct and pair questions. Easy = 0, Moderate = 1–2, Tough = 3 or more. The figures and negative-framing checks were done by pattern rules on the question text, so they are reproducible but can miss an unusual phrasing.

Traps. Each question was checked for the device that makes a wrong option attractive. Codes: same-family mismatch, location swap, fabricated claim, absolute qualifier, category swap, chronology slip, reversal, adjacent concept, number or threshold, wrong legal source, wrong cause, scope, best-fit among true options, qualifier shift, “defined in the Constitution”, part-for-whole, exception. A question can carry two traps; 566 carry none.

Repetition. Pairs of questions were linked as exact (same question), near (same fact, reworded), relationship (the same relationship asked from another subject or side) or conceptual (the same concept, different fact). Format repetition is described in the text of part 14.

Recurrence classes. Class A: topic present in 9 or more of the 17 papers; B: 5–8; C: 3–4; D: 1–2. The “since 2018” figure counts the eight papers that the UPPCS analysis also covers.

UPPCS comparison. The UPPCS side comes from the user’s UPPCS analysis (2018–2025, 1,200 questions): its topic tables and its sub-topic lists. That document gives topic counts, not question-level tags, so UPPCS evidence here is at topic level and is quoted as it appears there. Cross-exam classes: Common core, Common core + depth difference, UPSC-specific, UPPCS-specific, Current-dynamic.

Answer keys. Tagging does not depend on the keys, but the answer-pattern tables do. Where the compilation’s key is wrong on the facts, the corrected key is used and the question is listed in the appendix (30 questions).

BCharts

CWhat changed over seventeen years

  • Economy (310), Environment (290) and Polity (252) together make up 48% of the compilation. Economy led in every period until 2022–25, when Polity (15.8%), Environment (15.2%) and Economy (14.8%) became nearly equal.
  • Geography dipped to 7.3% in 2018–21 and came back to 14.5% in 2022–25; International Relations rose from 3.6% (2009–13) to 8.0% (2022–25).
  • The share of purely static questions fell from about 86–99% in 2010–2014 to 49–75% from 2016 onwards (49% in 2016, 53% in 2025). Most of the change is current-linked static questions, where the news is the trigger and static knowledge still decides the answer.
  • Formats changed sharply after 2021: “how many” questions went from none (2009–2021) to 8 (2022), 46 (2023), 17 (2024) and 25 (2025), and Statement-I/II questions from 1 (2021) to 18, 13 and 14 (2023–25). Direct questions fell from about 60% (2009–2011) to 17% in 2025.
  • On the UPPCS scale the mean difficulty score was 1.0–1.3 in most years before 2020 (2012 was higher, at 1.7) and about 1.5–1.7 in 2022–25, driven by format rather than obscurity. Twelve questions scored Tough in each of 2020 and 2025.
  • Peripheral-fact questions were few in 2011–2015 (5–13 a year) and have been 24–41 a year since 2016.

Bold marks each subject’s most frequent trap. A question can carry two traps.

  • Same-family mismatch is the commonest trap overall (247 questions): the wrong option is taken from the same family as the right one (the same decade, institution type or scheme family). It is the leading trap in Economy, Polity, Modern History, International Relations and Current Affairs.
  • Location swaps (147) concentrate in Geography (65), Ancient/Culture (23), Environment (26) and IR (14). In all four the wrong row is a neighbouring State or country.
  • Fabricated claims (172) are the main trap in statement questions on schemes, Acts and initiatives: a real object with one invented feature or purpose.
  • Category swaps (161) are the leading trap in Environment (41) and the second in Economy (40): an item put in the wrong class, account, list or Part.
  • Absolute qualifiers (93) are not automatically false. Most absolute statements checked were false, but true ones occur (“only some cyclones develop an eye”, “only government bodies can process monazite”).
  • Reversals (78) matter most where the subject has directions: Economy (30), Geography (13), Science (11).

International Relations & Organisations454 (9%)7 (16%)34
Current Affairs & Miscellaneous183 (17%)2 (11%)13
All subjects867166 (19%)68 (8%)633
  • In 867 statement, multi-select and pair questions with code options, every item was correct in 166 (19%) and no item was correct in 68 (8%). All-correct and none-correct answers are common enough that neither can be ruled out by habit.
  • In the 96 “how many” questions, the keyed answer was “only two” in 39, “all” in 19, “none” in 13, “only three” in 12, “only one” in 9 and “only four” in 4. This is a base rate, not a guessing rule.
  • Six statements (2019–23) said the Constitution “defines” or “classifies” something; all six were false.
  • Trend statements with “steadily” or “consistently” were false for growth rates, ratios and trade values, but true for a nominal level (GDP at market prices, 2015).

Exact and near repeats are rare (19 pairs in 1,759 questions). What recurs far more often is the territory: 152 of 605 fact clusters were asked in four or more papers. Each subject’s part 14 lists its pairs; part 5 lists its recurring territories.

Direct questions are still the largest block (72 of 154), but they have changed character: early direct questions asked for a location or a name, while recent ones use a description or a term (“correct description of paramitas”, “Vattakirutal”, “Kulah-Daran”). Since 2022 the pair questions have moved into the “how many pairs” form (6 HM-pair questions, all 2022–25), which removes the option of partial elimination. Statement questions (34) carry the doctrinal and chronological content: sect attribution, contemporaneity, causes.

2018–25 window on the UPPCS scale: Easy 32, Moderate 44, Tough 1 of 77. Mean score 1.10 (all years).

The UPPCS-equivalent score calls most of this subject Moderate because the formats are light. The real difficulty is obscurity: 70 of 154 questions (45%) are coded X (peripheral fact), the highest share among the nine static subjects (only Current Affairs is higher). Many of these are term or site items that no amount of reasoning can recover (Kulah-Daran, Nannuka, Sanghabhuti).

6Sub-topic and fact-cluster frequency

Every fact cluster asked more than once, grouped by topic. Years in brackets; ×2 means two questions in the same paper. One-off clusters are listed underneath each topic.

Buddhism & Jainism (doctrine, sects, sites) — 24 questions

  • Buddhism concepts (7) — Doctrinal vocabulary and iconography: nirvana (2013), Bhumisparsha mudra (2012), Maitreya (2018), paramitas and ascetic terms parivrajaka/shramana/upasaka (2020), epithets Shakyamuni/Tathagata vs Nayaputta (2024), reasons for decline (2010)2010, 2012, 2013, 2018, 2020 (×2), 2024
  • Jainism (6) — Jain doctrine and practice: anekantavada, universal law, karma-annihilation by penance, souls in rocks and water (2023 passage), Sthanakvasi sect, Mahamastakabhisheka of Bahubali2009 (×2), 2011, 2013, 2018, 2023
  • Buddhist sites (5) — Buddhist geography: kingdoms of the Buddha’s life (Kosala, Magadha — asked identically 2014 and 2015), Tabo in Spiti (2009, 2014), Dhanyakataka in Andhra (2023)2009, 2014 (×2), 2015, 2023
  • Buddhist sects (4) — Sect attribution: Bodhisattva as Mahayana (2016), Sautrantika/Sammitiya/Sarvastivadin (2017), Mahayana features (2019), Sthaviravadin and Lokottaravadin (2020)2016, 2017, 2019, 2020

Asked once: Buddhism jainism comparison (2012); Religious scholars (2022)

Economy, society & historical terminology — 20 questions

  • Historical terms (11) — Glossary of economic and administrative terms: Mahattara/Pattakila, Araghatta (2016, 2025), Maagadha, Banjaras, Vishti, Anurang/Banian/Mirasidar, kulyavapa/dronavapa, Hundi, Kulah-Daran, Fanam2014, 2016 (×3), 2019, 2020 (×3), 2022 (×2), 2025
  • Ancient trade ports (5) — Maritime trade and guilds: Bay of Bengal monsoon contacts, shreni guild rules, Motupalli (Kakatiya), Ghantasala–Kadura–Chaul (Gupta ports), Korkai–Poompuhar–Muchiri2011, 2012, 2017, 2020, 2023
  • Ancient society law (2) — Legal-social texts: Mitakshara vs Dayabhaga (2021), slavery in the Arthashastra (2022)2021, 2022

Asked once: Ancient science (2012); Feudalism land grants (2015)

Architecture & sculpture — 17 questions

  • Stupa cave architecture (6) — Rock-cut and stupa architecture: oldest caves (Barabar), chaitya vs vihara, Ajanta–Mahabalipuram commonality, Ajanta on the Waghora, stupa origin and function, Besnagar/Bhaja/Sittanavasal shrines2013 (×2), 2016, 2021, 2023 (×2)
  • Temple sites (5) — Temples and their location or builders: Virupaksha–Hampi, temple–state match (2009), Sun temples (Arasavalli), Chausath Yogini (Morena), Somnath2009 (×2), 2017, 2021, 2022
  • Temple styles (3) — Temple style vocabulary: Nagara/Dravida/Vesara, Panchayatan, Kalyana mandapa (Vijayanagara)2012, 2014, 2019
  • Sculpture (2) — Sculpture sites and relative dating: Mahaparinirvana image, Dhauli elephant as earliest2014, 2015

Asked once: Indo islamic architecture (2018)

Performing arts (music, dance, martial/folk) — 13 questions

  • Music traditions (6) — Hindustani/Carnatic traditions and figures: Gundecha brothers (Dhrupad), Dhrupad origin, Manganiyars, Tyagaraja kritis, Tansen, Paluskar’s Gandharva Mahavidyalaya (Lahore)2009, 2012, 2014, 2018, 2019, 2025
  • Dance traditions (4) — Classical dance features: Kuchipudi vs Bharatanatyam, Tribhanga, Sattriya, Manipuri Sankirtana2012, 2013, 2014, 2017
  • Martial folk arts (3) — Art form–state pairs: Gatka, Kalaripayattu, Garba/Mohiniattam/Yakshagana2009, 2014 (×2)

Literature, authors & foreign travellers — 10 questions

  • Ancient literature authors (6) — Text–author–patron matching: Malavikagnimitra, Panini/Amarasimha associations, Bhavabhuti/Hastimalla/Kshemeshvara (playwrights), Jaina texts, Devichandragupta/Hammira-Mahakavya/Milinda-panha, Bhasa2016, 2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024
  • Foreign travellers (4) — Travellers and what they recorded: Hiuen Tsang, Tavernier (diamonds), Sanghabhuti, Fa-hien under Chandragupta II2013, 2018, 2024, 2025

Painting, crafts & textiles — 9 questions

  • Crafts textiles GI (4) — Textile towns, GI tags and craft–state pairs: Chanderi/Kancheepuram, Banaras brocade and Tirupati laddu, Kalamkari, Puthukkuli/Sujni/Uppada2014, 2015 (×2), 2018
  • Mural cave painting (3) — Cave and mural painting: Bagh caves (Gupta), Ajanta/Lepakshi murals, Padmapani at Ajanta2010, 2013, 2017
  • Painting schools (2) — Painting schools: Bani Thani (Kishangarh), Jahangir’s shift to portraits and albums2018, 2019

Prehistory, chalcolithic & Harappan — 8 questions

  • Harappan (6) — Harappan features and sites: secular character, deities, Rigvedic vs Harappan contrasts, non-Harappan site (Sohgaura), Dholavira water management, Dancing Girl (bronze)2011, 2013, 2017, 2019, 2021, 2025
  • Prehistoric chalcolithic sites (2) — Site–feature–state triplets: Burzahom, Chandraketugarh, Ganeshwar, Inamgaon, Mangadu2021, 2024

Vedic age, Upanishads & philosophical schools — 7 questions

  • Philosophical schools (3) — Darshanas: Sankhya doctrine (2013), which pair is outside the six systems (2014), Ramanuja’s teaching (2022)2013, 2014, 2022
  • Vedic society religion (2) — Early Vedic religion and the dharma–rita idea2011, 2012
  • Vedic texts (2) — Vedic and Upanishadic texts: Satyameva Jayate from the Mundaka Upanishad, Upanishad parables and chronology2014, 2024

Mughals (polity, administration, culture) — 6 questions

  • Mughal culture (2) — Mughal cultural patronage: Ibadat Khana, Persian translation of Yogavasistha under Akbar2014, 2022
  • Mughal administration (2) — Mughal administrative structure: jagirdar vs zamindar, pargana–sarkar–suba2019, 2021

Asked once: Later mughals (2010); Mughal political (2015)

Festivals, calendar & UNESCO lists — 6 questions

  • UNESCO lists (3) — UNESCO World Heritage and Intangible Heritage inscriptions (Kalka–Shimla railway 2009; Garba and 2024 property list)2009, 2024 (×2)
  • Festivals traditions (2) — Festival or tradition–community/state pairs: Chaliha Sahib, Nanda Raj Jaat, Wari-Warkari; Chapchar Kut, Khongjom Parba, Thang-Ta2017, 2018

Asked once: Calendars eras (2014)

Bhakti & Sufi movements — 6 questions

  • Bhakti saints (5) — Bhakti saints: who was not a Bhakti proponent, contemporaneity with Babur and Akbar, Bijak and Pushti Marg attribution, Siddhas and Lingayats2010, 2013, 2014, 2016, 2019

Asked once: Sufism (2012)

Mahajanapadas, Mauryas & Ashokan inscriptions — 6 questions

  • Ashoka edicts (4) — Ashokan inscriptions: Prinsep’s decipherment, Kanganahalli portrait, edict text on sectarian tolerance, major rock edict sites by state2016, 2019, 2020, 2022

Asked once: Mauryan administration (2025); Mahajanapadas (2025)

Sangam & South Indian dynasties — 5 questions

  • Pallavas cholas (3) — Pallava–Chola polity: eripatti/taniyur/ghatika terms, Mahendravarman I’s titles, Rajendra I’s Srivijaya campaign2016, 2025 (×2)
  • Sangam (2) — Sangam literature: varna in Sangam poems, vattakirutal (ritual suicide)2022, 2023

Historical geography (places, regions, old names) — 5 questions

  • Places regions rivers (3) — Place–hill/region/river pairs: Srisailam–Nallamala, Khajuraho–Bundelkhand, Tirupati–Rayalaseema, Pandharpur–Chandrabhaga, Hampi–Tungabhadra2015, 2016, 2019
  • Ancient place names (2) — Old names to present regions: Champaka, Durgara, Kuluta; Bhilsa, Dwarasamudra, Girinagar, Sthanesvara2015, 2020

Vijayanagara — 5 questions

  • Vijayanagara (5) — Vijayanagara rulers and institutions: Harihara I’s founding, Krishnadevaraya’s taxation and Bhatkal fort, Devaraya I’s Tungabhadra dam, Nuniz on women2015, 2016, 2021, 2023, 2024

Post-Gupta & early-medieval dynasties — 4 questions

  • Early medieval dynasties (4) — Early-medieval dynasties and their relative chronology: Pratihara, Pallava, Chola, Chandela, Paramara; kingdoms between the Guptas and Harsha2020, 2021, 2022, 2023

Delhi Sultanate & Mongol invasions — 3 questions

  • Invasions synchronisms (2) — Who was ruling when: Chengiz Khan and Iltutmish; the first Mongol invasion2021, 2022

Asked once: Sultanate administration (2019)

7Question-generating concepts

The concepts that keep producing questions: what the examiner holds constant, and what changes between versions.

Doctrine and sect attribution

Concept
A religious or philosophical tradition is identified by its doctrine terms, sects, texts, epithets and sites; the examiner tests whether a term is assigned to the right tradition or sect.
Dimensions tested
term → meaning; term → tradition; sect → parent school; epithet → person; feature → Mahayana/Hinayana; practice common to Buddhism and Jainism
How the examiner varied it
Meaning of a term (DIR: 2012 mudra, 2013 nirvana, 2020 paramitas) → sect attribution in statements (2016 Bodhisattva, 2017 Sautrantika, 2020 Sthaviravadin) → epithet selection (2024) → cross-tradition how-many pairs (2022 Aryadeva/Dignaga/Nathamuni)
Facts that recur
Bodhisattva ideal = Mahayana; Sthaviravada = the Theravada line; Lokottaravadins came out of the Mahasanghikas; Sautrantika and Sammitiya are Buddhist schools; Sthanakvasi is a Jain sect; anekantavada is Jain; Nayaputta refers to Mahavira
Adjacent knowledge
Buddhist councils and schools; Jain sects (Digambara, Svetambara, Sthanakvasi, Terapanthi); Tirthankaras; Alvars, Nayanars and Virashaivas; six darshanas and the heterodox schools (Lokayata, Ajivika)

Historical terminology decoding

Concept
Terms for administration, land, revenue, money and occupations, with the period they belong to.
Dimensions tested
term → meaning; term → period; officials → level of administration; units in ascending order
How the examiner varied it
DIR “the term X refers to” (2014, 2016, 2020, 2022, 2025) → pair lists of terms (2016 Chola, 2020 Anurang/Banian/Mirasidar) → difference questions (2019 jagirdar vs zamindar) → order of units (2021 pargana–sarkar–suba)
Facts that recur
Araghatta = Persian-type water wheel (2016, 2025); kulyavapa/dronavapa = land measures; hundi = bill of exchange; vishti = forced labour as a form of tax; fanam = coin; Kulah-Daran = Sayyids; pradeshika/rajuka/yukta = Ashokan district-level officers
Adjacent knowledge
An era-wise glossary: Mauryan (samaharta, sannidhata), Gupta (bhukti, vishaya), Chola (ur, sabha, nadu), Sultanate (iqta, kharaj, diwan-i-arz), Mughal (mansab, zat/sawar, madad-i-maash), Vijayanagara (nayankara, amaram)

Site and place identification

Concept
A historical place is fixed by state, region, river, hill range or the feature it is known for.
Dimensions tested
site → state; site → feature; place → river; place → hill/region; old name → present place; port → dynasty
How the examiner varied it
Where is X (2009) → pairs (2014–2020) → three-column site/state/description (2024) → how-many pairs (2022 edict sites, 2025 Mahajanapada rivers)
Facts that recur
Dhauli and Jaugada in Odisha, Erragudi in Andhra Pradesh, Kalsi in Uttarakhand; Chandraketugarh in West Bengal; Dwarasamudra in Karnataka; Khajuraho in Bundelkhand; Tirupati in Rayalaseema; Hampi on the Tungabhadra
Adjacent knowledge
Maps of the Mahajanapadas, Ashokan edicts, Harappan sites by state, Buddhist sites and ports on both coasts; the modern-geography overlap is covered under Geography

Art-form attribution

Concept
A cultural form is identified by style, dynasty, site or defining feature.
Dimensions tested
feature → style; site → religion; painting → school; dance → distinguishing feature; musician → tradition
How the examiner varied it
Style names (2012 Nagara/Dravida/Vesara) → difference questions (2012 Kuchipudi vs Bharatanatyam; 2013 chaitya vs vihara) → statements with absolute qualifiers (2017 Manipuri Sankirtana, 2021 Chausath Yogini) → how-many site pairs (2023)
Facts that recur
Kalyana mandapa → Vijayanagara; Bagh caves → Gupta painting; Bani Thani → Kishangarh; the Barabar caves, not Badami, are the oldest rock-cut caves; dancing on a brass plate → Kuchipudi
Adjacent knowledge
A temple architecture schema; cave sites by religion; painting schools (Mughal, Rajput, Pahari, Deccani); the eight classical dances and their markers; Hindustani vs Carnatic

Chronology and synchronism

Concept
Placing rulers, saints, invasions and travellers on the same timeline.
Dimensions tested
who was contemporary with whom; which came first; which kingdoms existed between two points
How the examiner varied it
Plain sequence (2015 earliest sculpture, 2020 dynasty order) → contemporaneity statements (2013 Nanak and Babur, 2019 Nimbarka and Akbar, 2021 Iltutmish and Chengiz Khan) → how-many in a century (2023, answer “none”)
Facts that recur
Chengiz Khan reached the Indus under Iltutmish; Fa-hien visited under Chandragupta II; Nanak preached when the Lodis fell; Nimbarka was not Akbar’s contemporary
Adjacent knowledge
A synchronism table for North India, the Deccan and the South, 6th–13th centuries

Text–author–patron

Concept
A literary work is tied to its author, its patron or dynasty and its genre or religion.
Dimensions tested
work → author; author → court; text → religion; motto → source text
How the examiner varied it
DIR (2016 Malavikagnimitra, 2024 Bhasa) → statements on associations (2020) → MSEL of Jaina texts (2022) → how-many pairs (2023)
Facts that recur
Malavikagnimitra (Kalidasa) is about Agnimitra, son of the Sunga founder; Parishishtaparvan and the Trishashtilakshana Mahapurana are Jaina; Satyameva Jayate comes from the Mundaka Upanishad
Adjacent knowledge
Gupta-era literature; Jaina and Buddhist canons; medieval chronicles (the UPPCS emphasis)

8Trap and distractor analysis

Location swap23 · 15% of subject · 8% overall
Same-family mismatch21 · 14% of subject · 14% overall
Fabricated claim13 · 8% of subject · 10% overall
Chronology slip11 · 7% of subject · 3% overall
Category swap10 · 6% of subject · 9% overall
Adjacent concept7 · 5% of subject · 3% overall
Absolute qualifier5 · 3% of subject · 5% overall
Reversal / swapped roles3 · 2% of subject · 4% overall
Best-fit among true options2 · 1% of subject · 4% overall
Number / threshold1 · 1% of subject · 5% overall

59 of 154 questions (38%) had no identifiable trap: the right answer needed only the fact. Counts can exceed the number of questions because one question can carry two traps.

  • Location swaps (LOC, 23) are the commonest trap here: the item is real and the state or region is a plausible neighbour (Jaugada given as Madhya Pradesh, Chandraketugarh as Odisha, Mohiniattam as Odisha, Sujni as Maharashtra).
  • Same-family mismatch (MISM, 21): the wrong partner comes from the same universe (Bijak given to Dadu instead of Kabir; Pushti Marg to Madhvacharya instead of Vallabha; Milinda-panha to Nagarjuna).
  • Category swap (CAT, 10): a sect or text is put in the wrong tradition (Sthaviravadin as Mahayana, Sautrantika as Jain, Aryadeva as a Jaina scholar).
  • Fabricated firsts and causes (FAB, 13): confident but invented claims, such as Babur introducing gunpowder and the arch, Mitakshara as law for upper castes only, or Harappans building palaces and temples.
  • Plausible contemporaneity (CHRON, 11): two figures from roughly the same era paired wrongly (Kabir and Sirhindi, Nimbarka and Akbar, the first Mongol invasion under Jalaluddin).

11Common note-worthiness

CORE

Asked in both exams; belongs in the shared base notes.

  • Literature, authors & foreign travellers
  • Prehistory, chalcolithic & Harappan
  • Bhakti & Sufi movements
  • Mahajanapadas, Mauryas & Ashokan inscriptions
  • Sangam & South Indian dynasties
  • Vijayanagara
  • Delhi Sultanate & Mongol invasions

CORE + EXTENSION

Shared base, plus a layer that one exam goes deeper into.

  • Buddhism & Jainism (doctrine, sects, sites)
  • Economy, society & historical terminology
  • Architecture & sculpture
  • Performing arts (music, dance, martial/folk)
  • Vedic age, Upanishads & philosophical schools
  • Mughals (polity, administration, culture)
  • Post-Gupta & early-medieval dynasties

EXAM-SPECIFIC

Mostly one exam’s territory; a separate layer.

  • Painting, crafts & textiles
  • Festivals, calendar & UNESCO lists
  • Historical geography (places, regions, old names)

LOW-YIELD

Rare in both; a line or a table row, not a page.

None.

12Frequency is not importance

  • The Delhi Sultanate (3 UPSC questions in 17 years) has 11 UPPCS questions in 8 years, and the Mughals (6 UPSC) have 21, plus 7 on the Marathas and later Mughals. For a common notes base they are CORE despite the low UPSC count.
  • Historical terminology (11 questions across 6 papers) is more important than its size suggests, because each term is a single fact that can be asked in any format, and the 2016 Araghatta term came back in 2025.
  • Individual obscure items (Nannuka, Sanghabhuti, Kanganahalli) are low-yield as facts, but they show the territory UPSC draws from: inscriptions, lesser dynasties and Buddhist scholarship.

13Cross-subject links

  • Geography: historical places are asked with the river, hill range or region they sit in (Srisailam–Nallamala, Pandharpur–Chandrabhaga), and Mahajanapadas with their rivers.
  • Polity: the national motto’s source (Mundaka Upanishad) and Article 18 on titles, which Misc 2021 tested alongside the Padma awards.
  • Current affairs: UNESCO lists, GI tags and statue or temple inaugurations are the triggers.
  • Modern History: Prinsep’s decipherment (2016) and the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya (1901) sit at the boundary with colonial-era culture.

14Repeated facts vs repeated relationships

3 linked pairs: exact 1, near 1, conceptual 1. The later question is quoted; IDs are the compilation’s microtheme numbers.

15Elimination analysis

Statement, multi-select and pair questions (code options)Q%
Every item correct913
No item correct34
Some items correct5682
Total68100
“How many” questions: keyed answerQ
Only one–
Only two8
Only three–
All–
None1
Total9
  • Where a statement attributes a sweeping cause (“Babur’s arrival led to the introduction of gunpowder”), check it against broad chronology. Gunpowder, arch and dome all predate the Mughals, so the chronology alone rules it out.
  • In sect questions, fix the one anchor you know (Bodhisattva = Mahayana) and check each option code against it. Most option sets cannot survive one firm anchor.
  • The how-many pair format (6 questions since 2022) removes code elimination entirely; only full recall of each pair works.

16Priority architecture

P1 · Build first

  • Buddhism & Jainism doctrine, sects, sites
  • Historical terminology glossary by era
  • Architecture (temple styles, cave and stupa)
  • Harappan features and sites
  • Ashokan inscriptions and Mauryan administration
  • Bhakti saints (attribution and contemporaneity)

P2 · Build fully

  • Performing arts (dance features, music traditions)
  • Literature and foreign travellers
  • Vedic texts and philosophical schools
  • Mughal and Sultanate administration (UPPCS weight)
  • Vijayanagara
  • Sangam and Pallava–Chola

P3 · Build as tables

  • Crafts, GI and textiles
  • Historical geography (old names, place–region pairs)
  • Early-medieval dynasty chronology
  • Festivals and UNESCO lists

P4 · Keep light

  • Single obscure items (individual kings, monks, inscriptions) beyond the era glossary

17Subject summary

In numbers
154 questions (8.8% of the compilation) · static 94%, current-linked or dynamic 6% · recall 36%, concept 16%, application 2%, peripheral 45%
What the subject is
A culture-weighted subject. UPSC asks more about religion, art and terminology than about dynastic narrative, and 45% of questions turn on peripheral facts.
Common core
Religious traditions and sects; the era-wise glossary; architecture; Harappan and Mauryan sources; Bhakti.
UPSC extension
Art and culture depth (dance, music, painting, crafts), historical geography, obscure inscriptions and texts.
UPPCS extension
Mughals, Sultanate, Marathas, medieval chronicles and battles in sequence.
Dominant traps
Location swaps, same-family mismatches and invented causes.
How to build the notes
Build the notes as cross-tables (term × era, site × state × feature, text × author × religion) rather than dynasty narratives.

Modern History

133 of 1,759 questions · 7.6% of the compilation

Modern History is the most “direct” of the static subjects: 74 of 133 questions are direct. Many of them are cause or reason questions (“because”, “the reason for”, “significant aspect of”), which behave like best-fit questions rather than recall. Statement questions (30) focus on the provisions of Acts and plans and on movement programmes. “How many” formats reach Modern History only in 2024–25 (3 questions).

2018–25 window on the UPPCS scale: Easy 16, Moderate 39, Tough 2 of 57. Mean score 1.10 (all years).

Scored on the UPPCS scale this is an easy-to-moderate subject, and recall dominates (82 of 133). The difficulty comes from near-miss options: the wrong option is usually a real Act, leader or date from the same decade (MISM and CHRON together account for 42 traps).

5Recurring vs occasional topics

Class A — core recurring (9+ of 17 papers)

  • Gandhian phase & mass movements (13 of 17 papers · 27 Q · 5/8 since 2018)
  • Constitutional development (Acts, commissions, Constituent Assembly) (14 of 17 papers · 25 Q · 6/8 since 2018)
  • British administration, economy & policies (10 of 17 papers · 20 Q · 3/8 since 2018)
  • Early nationalism, INC & Swadeshi (11 of 17 papers · 14 Q · 4/8 since 2018)

Class B — regular (5–8 papers)

  • Personalities, organisations & books (7 of 17 papers · 10 Q · 4/8 since 2018)
  • Europeans & British expansion (8 of 17 papers · 10 Q · 5/8 since 2018)
  • Socio-religious reform & anti-caste movements (6 of 17 papers · 10 Q · 3/8 since 2018)
  • Revolutionary, leftist & labour movements (7 of 17 papers · 8 Q · 2/8 since 2018)

Class C — occasional (3–4 papers)

  • Tribal & peasant uprisings (4 of 17 papers · 4 Q · 2/8 since 2018)
  • Communal politics, INA & partition (3 of 17 papers · 3 Q · 1/8 since 2018)

Class D — rare (1–2 papers)

  • Post-independence politics (2 of 17 papers · 2 Q · 2/8 since 2018)

Every fact cluster asked more than once, grouped by topic. Years in brackets; ×2 means two questions in the same paper. One-off clusters are listed underneath each topic.

7Question-generating concepts

The concepts that keep producing questions: what the examiner holds constant, and what changes between versions.

Features of constitutional documents

Concept
Each Act, commission or plan has a small set of defining provisions, and the examiner asks which provision belongs to which document.
Dimensions tested
provision → document; document → what it “clearly defined”; residuary powers; reserved vs transferred subjects; carry-over into the 1950 Constitution
How the examiner varied it
Direct “the Act of 1919 clearly defined …” (2015) → feature multi-select (2012) → statements mixing true and false provisions (2019 Charter Act 1813, 2021 Montford franchise, 2024 Act of 1935) → reserved-subject list (2022) → A–R on Constitution Day (2023)
Facts that recur
Diarchy in provinces (1919); residuary powers with the Governor-General (1935); Instrument of Instructions → DPSP; Centre–State scheme from 1935; Cripps offered dominion status and a constitution-making body; Governor-General of India from the Charter Act 1833
Adjacent knowledge
Sources of the Constitution, Regulating Act 1773, Pitt’s India Act, Indian Councils Acts 1861/1892/1909, the August Offer, Wavell Plan, Mountbatten Plan, Indian Independence Act

Anatomy of a mass movement

Concept
Every movement can be described by trigger, programme, leaders, place, date and outcome.
Dimensions tested
trigger and cause; resolution date; programme items; regional leaders; underground actors; reasons for withdrawal
How the examiner varied it
Slogan or origin (2009) → regional organiser (2015 Rajagopalachari) → exact date and body (2021, 8 August AICC) → how-many programme items (2025 NCM)
Facts that recur
Quit India came after the Cripps failure; the AICC adopted the resolution on 8 August 1942; Usha Mehta ran the Congress Radio; Swadeshi and boycott were first used against the Bengal partition
Adjacent knowledge
Khilafat, Bardoli, Nagpur flag satyagraha, Vaikom, 1934 withdrawal of CDM, Individual Satyagraha order, INA trials, RIN mutiny

Colonial economy and administration

Concept
British policy explained through its instruments (settlements, charters, regulations) and consequences (drain, commercialisation, deindustrialisation).
Dimensions tested
system → features; system → official; policy → purpose; consequence → cause
How the examiner varied it
Direct cause questions (2011 litigation, 2020 indigo decline) → feature statements (2012 Ryotwari) → officials multi-select (2017 Read and Munro) → negative-feature statement (2024 “peasants exempted”)
Facts that recur
Ryotwari: direct payment, pattas, periodic revision; Permanent Settlement produced litigation over sale laws; Home Charges included pensions and interest; Naoroji, Dutt and Iyer as economic critics
Adjacent knowledge
Mahalwari (Holt Mackenzie), sunset law, Deccan Riots, famine commissions, railway guarantee system

Person → organisation → position

Concept
Matching individuals to the bodies they founded, led or served.
Dimensions tested
founder; first president; secretary; journal; descriptive identification
How the examiner varied it
Direct (2009 Hindu College) → multi-select (2012 Ambedkar parties, 2016 Keshab Chandra Sen) → pair lists (2017, 2019 ×2) → description paragraph (2018 Lajpat Rai)
Facts that recur
Radhakanta Deb first president of the British Indian Association; Sahajanand Saraswati and the Kisan Sabha; Ashok Mehta and others founded HMS; Rash Behari Bose linked to the Ghadar party
Adjacent knowledge
A table of 19th–20th century associations with founders, years, places and journals

European companies in India

Concept
The arrival, settlements and legacies of the Portuguese, Dutch, English and French.
Dimensions tested
factory location and date; granting authority; order of occupation; things introduced
How the examiner varied it
Direct (2009 Surat) → order of occupation (2010 Pondicherry) → location in a quarter-century (2021) → how many fruits (2025)
Facts that recur
English factory at Surat with Jahangir’s permission; Portuguese first at Pondicherry; Diu surrendered by Bahadur Shah of Gujarat; papaya, pineapple and guava came with the Portuguese
Adjacent knowledge
Carnatic wars, Battle of Bedara, farmans of 1717, Danish settlements

8Trap and distractor analysis

Same-family mismatch29 · 22% of subject · 14% overall
Fabricated claim16 · 12% of subject · 10% overall
Chronology slip13 · 10% of subject · 3% overall
Best-fit among true options8 · 6% of subject · 4% overall
Location swap7 · 5% of subject · 8% overall
Adjacent concept3 · 2% of subject · 3% overall
Category swap2 · 2% of subject · 9% overall
Scope / coverage2 · 2% of subject · 1% overall
Wrong cause–effect2 · 2% of subject · 2% overall
Reversal / swapped roles1 · 1% of subject · 4% overall
Number / threshold1 · 1% of subject · 5% overall

51 of 133 questions (38%) had no identifiable trap: the right answer needed only the fact. Counts can exceed the number of questions because one question can carry two traps.

  • Same-family mismatch (MISM, 29) dominates: the wrong option is a real person, body or Act from the same decade (the Nehru Report credited with a demand for complete independence; residuary powers under the 1935 Act given to the provinces).
  • Fabricated provisions (FAB, 16): invented clauses attached to real Acts, such as the Montford reforms giving votes to all women over 21, or the Factories Act 1881 fixing wages.
  • Chronology (CHRON, 13): exact dates and orders (8 August 1942, the annulment of the Bengal partition in 1911, the earliest of several events).
  • Best-fit reasons (BEST, 8): several options are true statements, and only one is the reason the question asks about (the Surat split, the National Social Conference, Cornwallis’s 1793 regulation).

11Common note-worthiness

CORE

Asked in both exams; belongs in the shared base notes.

  • Gandhian phase & mass movements
  • Early nationalism, INC & Swadeshi
  • Personalities, organisations & books
  • Europeans & British expansion
  • Socio-religious reform & anti-caste movements
  • Revolutionary, leftist & labour movements
  • Communal politics, INA & partition

CORE + EXTENSION

Shared base, plus a layer that one exam goes deeper into.

  • Constitutional development (Acts, commissions, Constituent Assembly)
  • British administration, economy & policies
  • Tribal & peasant uprisings

EXAM-SPECIFIC

Mostly one exam’s territory; a separate layer.

None.

LOW-YIELD

Rare in both; a line or a table row, not a page.

  • Post-independence politics

12Frequency is not importance

  • The 1857 revolt has 9 UPPCS questions (with tribal and peasant uprisings) and none in the 2009–25 UPSC compilation. It stays in the common notes because UPPCS asks it and UPSC can ask its administrative consequences (Queen’s Proclamation, 2014).
  • Constitutional development (25 UPSC questions) also carries Polity: the 1935 Act and the Constituent Assembly are asked in Polity papers too, so one set of notes serves both subjects and both exams.
  • Post-independence politics (2 questions) is low-yield for the notes, but both questions are sequencing or pairing items that reward a simple timeline.

13Cross-subject links

  • Polity: Acts of 1919/1935, Cripps and the Constituent Assembly feed directly into sources of the Constitution, DPSP and federal structure.
  • Economy: drain of wealth, land revenue systems and commercialisation are also economic-history questions; labour laws link to present labour codes.
  • Art & culture: reformers’ educational institutions and colonial archaeologists (2023) connect to cultural history.
  • Current affairs: national days (Constitution Day, Handloom Day) are used as hooks for Modern History facts.

14Repeated facts vs repeated relationships

2 linked pairs: near 1, conceptual 1. The later question is quoted; IDs are the compilation’s microtheme numbers.

TypeYearsWhat came back
Near2010 → 2016The ‘Swadeshi’ and ‘Boycott’ were adopted as methods of struggle for the first time during theM9.6 · M9.3
Conceptual2012 → 2014The 1929 Session of Indian National Congress is on significance in the history of the Freedom Movement because the:M5.7 · M5.4

Quit India (6 questions), the Cripps proposals (6) and the 1919 Act (6) recur as territories rather than as copied questions: each return asks a different dimension (slogan → organiser → date; offer → negotiators → Constituent Assembly composition).

15Elimination analysis

Statement, multi-select and pair questions (code options)Q%
Every item correct1020
No item correct24
Some items correct3876
Total50100
“How many” questions: keyed answerQ
Only one–
Only two1
Only three1
All1
None–
Total3
  • In “because” and “reason” questions, two or three options are usually true facts about the period. The key is the one that answers the causal question. Check each option against the question, not only against the facts.
  • Two direct questions (2011, 2012) keyed “none of the above”. In these reason questions the listed options were plausible but not the causes UPSC accepts, so “none” cannot be ruled out automatically.
  • In feature questions on Acts, fix one certain provision (diarchy = 1919; federation = 1935) and use it to strike codes.

16Priority architecture

P1 · Build first

  • Constitutional development: Acts 1773–1947, plans, Constituent Assembly
  • Gandhian movements (trigger, programme, dates, leaders)
  • Colonial economy: land revenue, drain, commercialisation
  • Socio-religious reform and anti-caste bodies
  • Early nationalism and Swadeshi

P2 · Build fully

  • Person–organisation tables
  • Europeans and factories
  • Labour, left and revolutionary movements
  • Tribal and peasant movements (+1857 for UPPCS)
  • Communal politics and partition

P3 · Build as tables

  • Colonial education institutions and founders
  • Books, journals and translations

P4 · Keep light

  • Post-independence event sequences

17Subject summary

In numbers
133 questions (7.6% of the compilation) · static 99%, current-linked or dynamic 1% · recall 62%, concept 13%, application 0%, peripheral 26%
What the subject is
A document- and movement-centred subject. UPSC asks what an Act provided, what a movement demanded, and who led which organisation.
Common core
Constitutional Acts and plans, Gandhian movements, the colonial economy, reform movements.
UPSC extension
Provision-level detail of Acts, economic-history reasoning, organisations and their first office-holders.
UPPCS extension
The 1857 revolt, wars of British expansion, Governor-General-wise policies, newspapers.
Dominant traps
Same-decade mismatches, invented provisions and exact dates.
How to build the notes
Keep one chronological spine (1757–1947) with Acts, movements and organisations attached to it; add feature tables for each Act.

Geography uses the widest spread of formats. Direct questions (76) and description-to-identification items (8) cover location and region recognition; multi-select (34) and two-statement (25) questions carry the physical-process content. All 12 of its Statement-I/II questions (A–R or three-statement) fall in 2023–25, almost always asking whether one physical fact explains another. How-many formats (16) are used for border and location lists.

2018–25 window on the UPPCS scale: Easy 21, Moderate 62, Tough 4 of 87. Mean score 1.15 (all years).

Concept and application questions make up 41% of Geography (82 of 202), the third-highest share after Science & Technology (64%) and Agriculture (43%). The UPPCS scale rates the A–R questions as Moderate to Tough, and that matches their real demand: both statements are usually true, and the task is to judge the explanation.

7Question-generating concepts

The concepts that keep producing questions: what the examiner holds constant, and what changes between versions.

Causal climatology

Concept
Atmospheric and oceanic patterns follow from a few mechanisms: how the air is heated, pressure belts, Earth’s rotation, and land–sea contrast.
Dimensions tested
why a pattern exists; which way a gradient runs; whether statement II explains statement I
How the examiner varied it
Why-multi-select (2011 jet aircraft, ionosphere; 2013 continentality) → paired statements on a phenomenon (2020 jet streams and cyclone eye, 2022 clouds, 2024 Coriolis) → Statement-I/II explanation (2023 land–sea contrast, 2024 troposphere thickness and heating, 2025 isotherms and dust)
Facts that recur
The atmosphere is heated mainly by terrestrial radiation; the troposphere is thicker at the equator because of convection; January isotherms bend equatorward over the northern continents; Coriolis force increases with wind speed and is greatest at the poles; southern westerlies are stronger because there is less land; cyclones are rare in the South Atlantic because the ITCZ seldom reaches it
Adjacent knowledge
Lapse rate and inversion, adiabatic change, air masses and fronts, local winds, jet-stream role in the monsoon, cloud types

Location and adjacency

Concept
A place is known by what surrounds it: neighbours, seas, straits, latitudes, ranges.
Dimensions tested
country ↔ sea or neighbour; latitude/longitude ↔ places; order along a direction; range ↔ countries
How the examiner varied it
Direct (2014 “Turkey lies between”) → which does not border (2015 Jordan) → multi-select of the same fact (2017) → sea–country pairs (2019) → how many countries border X (2023, 2024, 2025)
Facts that recur
Jordan has no Mediterranean coast; Afghanistan borders Tajikistan, Turkmenistan and Uzbekistan; only Germany and Norway of the 2024 list touch the North Sea; the Tropic of Capricorn crosses Australia, Namibia, Brazil and Chile
Adjacent knowledge
Map practice of conflict zones and chokepoints (see International Relations), landlocked countries, capitals that moved

River systems of India

Concept
Each river is fixed by its source, direction, tributaries, the landforms it makes and the projects on it.
Dimensions tested
source; direction and structural reason; tributary membership; projects and reservoirs; lakes and waterfalls; glacier source
How the examiner varied it
Origin (2009 Amarkantak) → why westward (2013 Narmada) → tributaries (2015 Godavari, 2016 Brahmaputra) → glacier–river pairs (2019) → reservoir–state and waterfall–region–river triplets (2022, 2024) → west-to-east order of Ganga tributaries (2024)
Facts that recur
Narmada flows through a rift valley; Indravati and Pranahita join the Godavari; Nagavali and Vamsadhara rise in the Eastern Ghats; Gandikota canyon is on the Pennar; Wular lies on the Jhelum; Gomati–Ghaghara–Gandak–Kosi from west to east
Adjacent knowledge
Interstate water disputes, river-linking links, national waterways, major dams by State

Resources and their legal status

Concept
Minerals and fuels asked from three sides: where they occur, how the law classifies them, and who leads world production.
Dimensions tested
occurrence; legal category and who auctions; quality characteristics; world leaders; uses
How the examiner varied it
Occurrence statements (2009 thorium, 2013 Gondwana/Kodarma) → why India imports coal (2012) → legal classification (2018 auctions, 2019 minor minerals, 2020 major minerals) → critical-mineral policy (2025)
Facts that recur
Monazite contains thorium and rare earths and only government bodies may process or export it; ilmenite and rutile give titanium; Indian coal is high-ash and low-sulphur; sand is a minor mineral; the DRC produces about three-quarters of the world’s cobalt
Adjacent knowledge
MMDR Act and its amendments, the critical minerals list, KABIL, the Minerals Security Partnership, rare-earth supply chains

Identify the region from its description

Concept
A paragraph of climate, vegetation or economic traits points to one region or State.
Dimensions tested
temperature regime; rainfall season; vegetation; livelihood; latitude
How the examiner varied it
Climate table (2010) → descriptive paragraphs (2011 Gujarat, 2012 Arunachal, 2013 steppe, 2015 equatorial, 2021 rainforest, 2024 marine west coast)
Facts that recur
Equatorial: afternoon convectional rain every day; Mediterranean: winter rain, evergreen oak; marine west coast: low annual range with rain all year
Adjacent knowledge
Köppen classes with a signature trait each; a one-line profile of each Indian State

Earth–Sun geometry

Concept
Day length, seasons and time follow from the tilt of the axis and from longitude.
Dimensions tested
solstice and equinox effects; day length by latitude; longitude and time; the date line
How the examiner varied it
Cause (2013 tilted axis) → what happens on 21 June (2019, 2022, 2024) → time zones and date line (2025)
Facts that recur
On 21 June the Sun does not set at the Arctic Circle; the Tropic of Cancer and the Arctic Circle get more than 12 hours of daylight; Delhi and Bengaluru are close in longitude
Adjacent knowledge
Standard time and IST, twilight, heat zones, the analemma

8Trap and distractor analysis

Location swap65 · 32% of subject · 8% overall
Wrong cause–effect16 · 8% of subject · 2% overall
Fabricated claim16 · 8% of subject · 10% overall
Reversal / swapped roles13 · 6% of subject · 4% overall
Category swap12 · 6% of subject · 9% overall
Number / threshold12 · 6% of subject · 5% overall
Absolute qualifier11 · 5% of subject · 5% overall
Adjacent concept6 · 3% of subject · 3% overall
Same-family mismatch6 · 3% of subject · 14% overall
Best-fit among true options2 · 1% of subject · 4% overall
Chronology slip2 · 1% of subject · 3% overall
Wrong legal source1 · 0% of subject · 1% overall

54 of 202 questions (27%) had no identifiable trap: the right answer needed only the fact. Counts can exceed the number of questions because one question can carry two traps.

  • Location swaps (LOC, 65) are by far the most common trap here, and 65 of the 147 location traps in the whole compilation. The wrong answer is always a real feature one row off (Namcha Barwa given to the Garhwal Himalaya, Ghataprabha to Telangana, Mallorca to Italy).
  • Reversals (REV, 13) invert a physical relationship: high and low clouds swapped, solar and terrestrial heating swapped, the season of the greatest land–sea contrast swapped.
  • Wrong cause (CAUSE, 16) is the trap in A–R questions: both statements are true, but the second does not explain the first.
  • Absolute qualifiers (ABS, 11): “no east-flowing rivers in Kerala”, “India has no thorium”, “none of them uses seawater”, “Himalayas in five States only”.
  • Numeric thresholds (NUM, 12): the freshwater share, IMR within one month, the OMT depth, the number of time zones.

11Common note-worthiness

CORE

Asked in both exams; belongs in the shared base notes.

  • India: drainage, lakes & river projects
  • World regional & locational geography
  • India: minerals, energy & industry
  • India: physiography, islands & political map
  • Geomorphology & earth history
  • Oceanography & hydrosphere
  • World economic geography (resources)
  • India: transport & infrastructure
  • India: climate, soils & natural vegetation
  • Human geography: tribes & communities

CORE + EXTENSION

Shared base, plus a layer that one exam goes deeper into.

  • Climatology (atmosphere, heat budget, winds, ENSO)
  • Climatic regions & biomes
  • Earth in space: latitudes, seasons, time
  • Population & demography

EXAM-SPECIFIC

Mostly one exam’s territory; a separate layer.

None.

LOW-YIELD

Rare in both; a line or a table row, not a page.

  • Polar research

12Frequency is not importance

  • World regional geography has 39 UPPCS questions in eight years against 24 UPSC questions in seventeen; the UPPCS extension is volume, not a different kind of question.
  • Uttar Pradesh geography (12 UPPCS questions) is purely UPPCS and belongs in its own layer.
  • Climatic regions have only 13 questions, but the description-to-region format (climate regions and States) appeared in 2010, 2011, 2012, 2013, 2015, 2021 and 2024, so one clear Köppen table and one State-profile table are high value.
  • Polar research (1 question) is low-yield as a topic, but polar stations are also asked under International Relations and Science.

13Cross-subject links

  • Environment: biomes, forest types, mangroves and rainforest soils overlap with ecology; methane hydrates and critical minerals link to climate and energy.
  • Agriculture: soils (red, laterite, black), crop geography and irrigation.
  • Polity: the minor-minerals question was asked in Geography (2019) and in Polity (2025), as the same relationship.
  • International Relations: places in the news are asked as location questions (see the IR section).
  • History: historical sites are asked with rivers and hills (Hampi–Tungabhadra, Srisailam–Nallamala).

14Repeated facts vs repeated relationships

6 linked pairs: near 1, relationship 1, conceptual 4. The later question is quoted; IDs are the compilation’s microtheme numbers.

TypeYearsWhat came back

Relationship2019 → 2025Consider the following statements: Statement I: In India, State Governments have no power for making rules for grant of…G5.9 · P16.1
Conceptual2010 → 2013On the planet earth, most of the freshwater exists as ice caps and glaciers. Out of the remaining freshwater, the…G9.8 · G9.7
Conceptual2011 → 2023Consider the following statements Statement-I: The soil in tropical rain forests is rich in nutrients. Statement-II:…V19.4 · G6.5
Conceptual2013 → 2021With reference to the water on the planet Earth, consider the following statements: 1. The amount of water in the…G9.7 · G9.2
Conceptual2021 → 2023Consider the following statements Statement-I: The soil in tropical rain forests is rich in nutrients. Statement-II:…G4.3 · G6.5

Geography repeats relationships more than facts: rainforest soils are nutrient-poor (2011 → 2021 → 2023) and freshwater storage shares (2010 → 2013 → 2021) come back as different questions on the same relationship.

15Elimination analysis

Statement, multi-select and pair questions (code options)Q%
Every item correct1416
No item correct1113
Some items correct6171
Total86100
“How many” questions: keyed answerQ
Only one1
Only two5
Only three1
All1
None5
Total16
  • Absolute words are not an automatic kill. Across the 11 absolute-hinged geography questions, 16 absolute-worded statements were false and 3 were true (“only some cyclones develop an eye”, “only government bodies can process or export monazite”, “Pulicat Lake is spread over two States only”).
  • In Statement-I/II questions, first decide each statement on its own; then ask whether II is the mechanism behind I. The traps sit in the second step.
  • In border and location lists, one confident map fact usually fixes the count; the how-many format still needs every item checked.

16Priority architecture

P1 · Build first

  • Climatology mechanisms (heating, pressure, winds, ENSO/IOD)
  • Indian rivers, lakes, reservoirs, waterfalls
  • World location: borders, seas, straits
  • Minerals: occurrence, law, critical minerals
  • Indian physiography and islands

P2 · Build fully

  • Climatic regions and biomes (description table)
  • Earth–Sun geometry and time
  • Geomorphology and earth history
  • Population indicators
  • Ocean currents and water distribution
  • Forest types and soils
  • World resource leaders

P3 · Build as tables

  • Transport infrastructure
  • Tribes and communities
  • UP geography (UPPCS layer)

P4 · Keep light

  • Polar research
  • Hurricane names and other one-off current items

17Subject summary

In numbers
202 questions (11.5% of the compilation) · static 86%, current-linked or dynamic 14% · recall 44%, concept 30%, application 11%, peripheral 15%
What the subject is
The most map-dependent and the most mechanism-dependent subject at once: half the questions ask where something is, the other half ask why something happens.
Common core
Indian drainage and physiography, climatology, world location, minerals.
UPSC extension
Causal Statement-I/II questions, legal classification of minerals, description-to-region identification.
UPPCS extension
Uttar Pradesh geography, census and settlement data, larger world-regional volume, passes.
Dominant traps
Location swaps (one row off), reversed relationships, true-but-not-the-cause.
How to build the notes
Build map sheets (rivers, borders, seas, ranges) and one mechanism sheet per physical system, each mechanism written as cause → effect.

Polity is the statement subject: 107 of 252 questions are two-statement or three-plus statement, and 34 more are multiple-select. Direct questions (93) split into two groups: “which part of the Constitution enshrines X” and best-fit definitions in political theory. How-many formats arrive in 2023 (14 questions: 8 in 2023, 6 in 2025, none in 2024) and are used for bodies, provisions and office-holder rules.

2018–25 window on the UPPCS scale: Easy 25, Moderate 84, Tough 16 of 125. Mean score 1.29 (all years).

Polity is mostly recall (147 of 252), but the recall is exact: the difficulty is in the qualifiers (“only”, “shall”, “must”, “by the Constitution”) rather than in obscurity. The UPPCS scale places most questions at Moderate because of the statement format.

7Question-generating concepts

The concepts that keep producing questions: what the examiner holds constant, and what changes between versions.

Where is it enshrined

Concept
Every principle sits in one place: Preamble, a Part, an Article, a Schedule, or only in a statute.
Dimensions tested
principle → Part (FR, DPSP, FD, Preamble); right → Article; subject → List; body → constitutional or statutory
How the examiner varied it
Single-answer “enshrined in” (2013 economic justice, 2014 international peace, 2015 and 2020 welfare state, 2020 separation of judiciary, 2021 concentration of wealth) → Article number (Article 21: 2018, 2019, 2021, 2024) → how-many provision–Part pairs (2025)
Facts that recur
Welfare state, separation of the judiciary and prevention of concentration of wealth are in the DPSP; privacy and marriage choice are read into Article 21; sovereignty, unity and integrity is also a Fundamental Duty; inter-State quarantine is in the Union List
Adjacent knowledge
Article map of Parts III, IV, IVA; the Seventh Schedule lists; statutory rights (RTE, RTI, consumer rights)

Parliamentary procedure

Concept
Rules for how bills move and how the Houses are run.
Dimensions tested
bill type and its route; lapse and joint sitting; presiding officers; committees; devices; sessions
How the examiner varied it
Direct (2013 Rajya Sabha amendments to a Money Bill, 2015 joint-sitting majority) → exception statements (2012, 2016, 2024 bills that lapse or go to a joint sitting) → qualifier statements on the Speaker (2012, 2022, 2024, 2025)
Facts that recur
Joint sitting is for ordinary bills, passed by a simple majority of those present and voting; a bill pending in the Rajya Sabha that the Lok Sabha has not passed does not lapse on dissolution; the Estimates Committee is the largest; the Speaker stays in office until the new House meets
Adjacent knowledge
Articles 107–122, rules of procedure, Leader of Opposition, ordinance-making under Article 123

Political concepts by best fit

Concept
Definitions of constitutional and political ideas, asked with several plausible options.
Dimensions tested
definition; relationship between two concepts; best safeguard; defining feature
How the examiner varied it
Statements (2013, 2014) → single best-fit definitions (six in 2017; then 2018, 2019, 2020, 2021, 2023) → comparison with the British model (2021)
Facts that recur
Parliamentary government = executive responsible to the lower House; constitutional government = limited government; liberty = the opportunity to develop oneself; best safeguard of liberty = separation of powers; rights are correlative with duties; rights are claims against the State
Adjacent knowledge
Rule of law (Dicey), types of democracy, justice (Rawls), sovereignty, secularism in the Indian sense

Office-holder rules

Concept
Appointment, eligibility, tenure, removal, immunity and duties of constitutional office-holders.
Dimensions tested
who appoints or removes; eligibility; tenure and vacancy; immunity; who can speak or appear
How the examiner varied it
Direct (2012 PM at appointment, 2013 Attorney General in the Lok Sabha) → paired statements (2016 Chief Secretary, 2018 Governor immunity) → how-many (2023 presidential election) → pardon and ordinance limits (2025)
Facts that recur
The PM need not be a member of either House when appointed; the AG can speak in the Lok Sabha but cannot vote; no criminal proceedings lie against a Governor during the term; an Ordinance cannot abridge a Fundamental Right
Adjacent knowledge
Articles 52–78, 153–167, 76, 165, 124–128, 148, 324

Federal and local structure

Concept
How power is divided among the Union, the States, local bodies and Scheduled Areas.
Dimensions tested
legislative relations; emergency consequences; fiscal transfers; Panchayat provisions; Fifth-Schedule consequences
How the examiner varied it
Direct on powers (2013 treaties, 2016 Rajya Sabha resolution) → purpose and consequences of the Fifth Schedule (2015, 2019, 2022, 2023, 2025) → Finance Commission criteria (2015, 2023, 2025) → minerals A–R (2025)
Facts that recur
Parliament can legislate for treaties without State consent; a two-thirds Rajya Sabha resolution lets Parliament legislate on a State subject; Fifth Schedule status protects tribal land from transfer; the 15th Finance Commission used demographic performance, forest and ecology, and tax effort
Adjacent knowledge
Articles 245–263, 280, 356; the 73rd/74th Amendments; the Sixth Schedule councils

Constitutional vs statutory

Concept
Whether a body, right or rule comes from the Constitution or from an ordinary law.
Dimensions tested
body status; right status; where a procedure is detailed; what the Constitution does not define
How the examiner varied it
Which bodies are not in the Constitution (2013) → property as a legal right (2021) → statute vs Constitution statements (2019 judges, 2022 contempt, 2022 ministers’ ranks) → how many are constitutional (2023, 2025)
Facts that recur
NDC, Planning Commission and Zonal Councils are not constitutional; impeachment details are in the Judges (Inquiry) Act 1968; contempt is defined in the 1971 Act; the ranks of ministers are not in the Constitution
Adjacent knowledge
NCBC (constitutional since 2018), NHRC and Lokpal (statutory), Inter-State Council (Article 263)

8Trap and distractor analysis

Same-family mismatch48 · 19% of subject · 14% overall
Fabricated claim35 · 14% of subject · 10% overall
Category swap29 · 12% of subject · 9% overall
Number / threshold23 · 9% of subject · 5% overall
Best-fit among true options21 · 8% of subject · 4% overall
Absolute qualifier18 · 7% of subject · 5% overall
Adjacent concept17 · 7% of subject · 3% overall
Qualifier shift16 · 6% of subject · 1% overall
Reversal / swapped roles9 · 4% of subject · 4% overall
Chronology slip8 · 3% of subject · 3% overall
“Defined in” trap6 · 2% of subject · 0% overall
Wrong legal source6 · 2% of subject · 1% overall
Scope / coverage6 · 2% of subject · 1% overall
Exception5 · 2% of subject · 0% overall
Part for whole2 · 1% of subject · 0% overall
Location swap2 · 1% of subject · 8% overall

34 of 252 questions (13%) had no identifiable trap: the right answer needed only the fact. Counts can exceed the number of questions because one question can carry two traps.

  • Same-family mismatch (MISM, 48): the right power with the wrong office (the Department of Revenue said to prepare the Budget; the Advocate General said to be appointed by the President).
  • “Defined in the Constitution” (DEF, 6): six statements between 2019 and 2023 claimed that the Constitution defines or classifies something (basic structure, office of profit, incapacity of judges, civil and criminal contempt, ranks of ministers, efficiency of administration). All six were false.
  • Qualifier shifts (QUAL, 16): “shall” for “may”, “must” for “can”, “any” for “some”. Four of these fall in 2024–25.
  • Exceptions (EXC, 5): which bills lapse, which bills go to a joint sitting.
  • Category swaps (CAT, 29): a principle placed in the wrong Part, or a statutory body called constitutional.

11Common note-worthiness

CORE

Asked in both exams; belongs in the shared base notes.

  • Parliament
  • Union executive
  • Fundamental Rights & writs
  • DPSP & Fundamental Duties
  • Schedules, Parts & provision mapping
  • State executive & legislature
  • Amendments & basic structure
  • Panchayati Raj & municipalities
  • Non-constitutional & statutory bodies
  • Elections & representation
  • Federalism, Centre–State relations & emergency
  • Constitutional bodies
  • Preamble & constitutional status
  • Citizenship & States

CORE + EXTENSION

Shared base, plus a layer that one exam goes deeper into.

  • Governance: regulators, agencies, standards, digital ID
  • Judiciary & legal services
  • Scheduled & tribal areas (Fifth/Sixth Schedule, PESA)

EXAM-SPECIFIC

Mostly one exam’s territory; a separate layer.

  • Political theory & constitutional concepts
  • Statutory law & rights legislation

LOW-YIELD

Rare in both; a line or a table row, not a page.

None.

12Frequency is not importance

  • Political theory (22 questions) has no UPPCS counterpart, yet it appeared in 10 papers, with 6 questions in 2017 alone and at least one every year from 2017 to 2021. It is UPSC-specific but high priority.
  • Scheduled Areas (9 questions) look small, but the Fifth Schedule alone was asked in five papers between 2015 and 2025, mostly about what notification does to tribal land.
  • Panchayati Raj has only 9 UPSC questions but 18 in UPPCS; for a common base it is CORE.
  • Regulators and agencies (12 questions) are high-volume but low-transfer: each item is a separate fact, and the list changes with new bodies.

13Cross-subject links

  • Modern History: the 1919 and 1935 Acts, Cripps and the Constituent Assembly are the background to federal structure, DPSP and the Instrument of Instructions.
  • Economy: Finance Commission, Money Bills, the budget, GST Council and Consolidated Fund are asked in both subjects.
  • Geography: minor minerals (Geography 2019 → Polity 2025).
  • Environment: Fifth Schedule, PESA and forest rights connect with forest governance.
  • Governance items overlap with Economy schemes and with current affairs.

15Elimination analysis

Statement, multi-select and pair questions (code options)Q%
Every item correct2417
No item correct1813
Some items correct9970
Total141100
“How many” questions: keyed answerQ
Only one5
Only two4
Only three1
All2
None2
Total14
  • Statements that say the Constitution “defines” or “classifies” a term were false in all 6 cases (2019–23). Treat such a statement as suspect unless you can name the Article.
  • Where a statement puts a procedural detail in the Constitution, check whether it actually sits in a statute or in the rules of procedure. The SRC trap turned on this in 6 questions.
  • Best-fit theory questions: eliminate options that describe a consequence or an instrument rather than the defining principle.

16Priority architecture

P1 · Build first

  • Parliament: procedure, officers, committees
  • Fundamental Rights (Article map, Article 21, writs)
  • DPSP and Fundamental Duties

  • Judiciary and legal services
  • Political theory concepts
  • Scheduled Areas and PESA
  • Schedules and provision mapping

P2 · Build fully

  • State executive and Governor
  • Amendments and basic structure
  • Panchayati Raj and municipalities
  • Federal relations and emergency
  • Constitutional bodies (Finance Commission, ECI, CAG)
  • Non-constitutional bodies and status
  • Elections and delimitation
  • Preamble
  • Governance bodies and standards

P3 · Build as tables

  • Statutory rights legislation
  • Citizenship and States

P4 · Keep light

  • Headquarters and chairpersons of individual agencies

17Subject summary

In numbers
252 questions (14.3% of the compilation) · static 91%, current-linked or dynamic 9% · recall 58%, concept 23%, application 2%, peripheral 16%
What the subject is
The most statement-heavy subject; it rewards exact knowledge of who does what under which provision, and it penalises loose qualifiers.
Common core
Parliament, Fundamental Rights, DPSP, Union executive, judiciary, schedules.
UPSC extension
Political theory, Scheduled Areas, statute-vs-Constitution distinctions, regulators and agencies.
UPPCS extension
Panchayati Raj volume, Article-number recall, constitutional bodies and reorganisation history.
Dominant traps
Wrong office for the right power, “defined in the Constitution”, qualifier shifts.
How to build the notes
Organise by institution (who, how appointed, powers, limits, source Article or statute), with a separate provision-to-Part index and a short theory glossary.

Economy is the largest subject (310). Direct questions (129) are mostly term-definition and direction-of-effect items; statement questions (108 two- and three-plus) test institutional fine print; multiple-select (49) is used for “which of these would increase/reduce X”. Statement-I/II questions (10, from 2023) test causal links in finance and trade. Two 2025 questions required arithmetic on deficits, a new form.

7Question-generating concepts

The concepts that keep producing questions: what the examiner holds constant, and what changes between versions.

Monetary transmission

Concept
An RBI action changes bank liquidity, then credit, then interest rates, then demand and prices.
Dimensions tested
what an instrument is; which direction it moves liquidity; effect on lending, money supply, inflation, growth; sterilisation
How the examiner varied it
Definitions (2010 SLR, 2013 OMO and bank rate) → direction (2014 lower interest rates, 2015 SLR cut) → what an expansionary RBI would not do (2020) → money multiplier (2019, 2021 identical) → Statement-I/II on post-pandemic hikes (2023)
Facts that recur
A CRR increase leaves banks less to lend; an SLR cut lets banks lower lending rates; OMO purchases add money; sterilisation uses OMOs; the multiplier rises with the banking habit; a cash withdrawal leaves money supply unchanged
Adjacent knowledge
Repo/reverse repo/SDF, LAF corridor, M0–M3, MPC structure and inflation target, liquidity coverage

Budget classification and deficit arithmetic

Concept
Every receipt and outlay is revenue or capital, and every deficit is a specific difference.
Dimensions tested
classification; deficit definitions; how deficits are financed; fiscal rules
How the examiner varied it
Deficit financing meaning (2011, 2013) → how to cut a persistent deficit (2015, 2016) → classification statements (2022, 2025) → arithmetic (2025: revenue, fiscal and primary deficits from given figures)
Facts that recur
Borrowing is a capital receipt; capital receipts create a liability or reduce assets; fiscal deficit = total expenditure − revenue receipts − non-debt capital receipts; primary deficit = fiscal deficit − interest payments
Adjacent knowledge
Effective revenue deficit, the FRBM path, Consolidated Fund/Contingency Fund/Public Account, off-budget borrowing

External-sector accounting

Concept
Cross-border flows sit in the current or capital account, and the exchange rate adjusts to them.
Dimensions tested
account classification; FDI vs FPI; reserve composition; exchange-rate indices; crisis vulnerability
How the examiner varied it
Account composition (2013 capital account, 2014 current account) → FDI traits (2012, 2020, 2021) → exchange-rate indices (2022 NEER/REER) → capital flight (2022) → trade-data Statement-I/II (2023, 2024)
Facts that recur
FDI is a non-debt-creating flow; reserves = foreign-currency assets, gold, SDRs and the IMF reserve tranche; a rise in NEER means the rupee has appreciated; Fed tightening can cause capital flight
Adjacent knowledge
CAD financing, ECBs, LRS, convertibility, the RBI’s forex intervention

Financial instruments and markets

Concept
Each instrument has a maturity, an issuer, a regulator and a risk profile.
Dimensions tested
money vs capital market; issuer; who can trade; regulator; return vs risk
How the examiner varied it
Term (2010 bear, 2014 venture capital, 2023 beta) → bond types (2016 masala, 2022 convertible and inflation-indexed) → market classification (2023, 2024 CBLO) → Statement-I/II on bond risk (2024, 2025)
Facts that recur
CBLO and call money are money-market instruments; masala bonds are rupee-denominated and issued abroad; credit rating agencies are regulated by SEBI; bondholders rank ahead of shareholders
Adjacent knowledge
SEBI vs RBI jurisdiction, AIFs, REITs, derivatives, the corporate bond market

Term → institution

Concept
A term, instrument or report is attributed to the body that owns it.
Dimensions tested
term → WTO/IMF/World Bank; report → publisher; bank → founder
How the examiner varied it
Direct (2009 NAMA-11, 2010 SSM, 2015 peace clause, 2016 boxes, 2020 gold tranche, 2022 RFI/RCF) → publisher questions (2009–2019, eight of them)
Facts that recur
Amber/blue/green boxes and the peace clause belong to the WTO AoA; SDR, gold tranche, RFI and RCF belong to the IMF; IDA is administered by the IBRD; the WEF publishes the Global Gender Gap and Competitiveness reports
Adjacent knowledge
WTO agreements list; IMF facilities; World Bank group entities; a report → publisher table

Scheme design

Concept
A scheme is asked through its purpose, target group, ministry, eligibility and funding.
Dimensions tested
objective; beneficiaries and age limits; ministry; contribution and guarantee; whether participation is mandatory
How the examiner varied it
Purpose (2015 Jan Dhan, 2016 MUDRA and UDAY) → statement sets on design (2016 APY and PMFBY, 2017 NSQF) → how-many on health interventions (2023)
Facts that recur
APY is a guaranteed minimum pension mainly for the unorganised sector; ABDM is voluntary for hospitals; JSY is a safe-motherhood intervention with cash assistance; MGNREGA covers adult members of any rural household
Adjacent knowledge
A scheme grid (ministry, launch year, target, key numbers); overlap with Polity governance and with UPPCS scheme questions

8Trap and distractor analysis

Same-family mismatch60 · 19% of subject · 14% overall
Category swap40 · 13% of subject · 9% overall
Reversal / swapped roles30 · 10% of subject · 4% overall
Fabricated claim27 · 9% of subject · 10% overall
Absolute qualifier27 · 9% of subject · 5% overall
Number / threshold21 · 7% of subject · 5% overall
Adjacent concept18 · 6% of subject · 3% overall
Scope / coverage12 · 4% of subject · 1% overall
Best-fit among true options12 · 4% of subject · 4% overall
Chronology slip4 · 1% of subject · 3% overall
Wrong cause–effect4 · 1% of subject · 2% overall
Wrong legal source3 · 1% of subject · 1% overall
Location swap2 · 1% of subject · 8% overall
Exception1 · 0% of subject · 0% overall
Qualifier shift1 · 0% of subject · 1% overall

56 of 310 questions (18%) had no identifiable trap: the right answer needed only the fact. Counts can exceed the number of questions because one question can carry two traps.

  • Same-family mismatch (MISM, 60) is the largest count of any one trap in any subject: the right term with the wrong institution (credit rating agencies under the RBI; the Budget prepared by the Department of Revenue).
  • Reversals (REV, 30): the direction of an effect flipped (inflation said to benefit bond-holders; a higher NEER read as depreciation).
  • Category errors (CAT, 40): an item placed in the wrong account, market or sector (remittances in the capital account; stock market as a money market).
  • Trend words (ABS, 27): “steadily increased” and “consistently increased” statements were false for growth rates, tax ratios and trade values (2011, 2015, 2017, 2020) but true for nominal GDP at market prices (2015).

11Common note-worthiness

CORE

Asked in both exams; belongs in the shared base notes.

  • Government schemes & programmes
  • Basic concepts, national income & growth
  • Industry, labour & infrastructure
  • Inflation & price indices
  • Indices & reports
  • Planning & liberalisation
  • Poverty, inclusion & employment

CORE + EXTENSION

Shared base, plus a layer that one exam goes deeper into.

  • Banking & financial inclusion
  • International economic institutions & trade agreements
  • Money, RBI & monetary policy
  • External sector (BoP, FDI/FPI, exchange rate, trade)
  • Budget & fiscal policy

EXAM-SPECIFIC

Mostly one exam’s territory; a separate layer.

  • Financial markets & instruments
  • Taxation
  • Digital payments

LOW-YIELD

Rare in both; a line or a table row, not a page.

None.

12Frequency is not importance

  • Schemes (37 UPSC, 31 UPPCS) are the largest common block, but most items are single facts that do not transfer between schemes; a grid is enough.
  • Indices and reports are 28 UPPCS questions against 10 UPSC; the UPSC questions stopped after 2019, so this is mainly a UPPCS layer.
  • Monetary instruments and the multiplier are fewer in number but carry the causal logic needed for many other questions (inflation, bonds, exchange rate).
  • Deficit arithmetic appeared only in 2025 (2 questions); it is new, not yet a trend.

13Cross-subject links

  • Polity: Money Bills, the budget process, Finance Commission and GST Council.
  • Agriculture: MSP, procurement, fertiliser subsidies and agricultural credit are analysed under Agriculture.
  • International Relations: WTO, IMF, World Bank and regional trade blocs overlap with international organisations.
  • Science & Technology: digital payments, blockchain, cryptocurrency and CBDC.
  • Geography: oil benchmarks and critical minerals are resource-economics items.

14Repeated facts vs repeated relationships

5 linked pairs: exact 3, near 1, conceptual 1. The later question is quoted; IDs are the compilation’s microtheme numbers.

TypeYearsWhat came back

Exact2013 → 2021Which one of the following is likely to be the most inflationary in its effects?E10.7 · E10.3
Exact2019 → 2021The money multiplier in an economy increases with which one of the following?E1.13 · E1.9
Near2015 → 2016There has been a persistent deficit budget year after year. Which action/ actions of the following can be taken by the…E2.19 · E2.17
Conceptual2012 → 2023Consider the following statements in relation to Janani Suraksha Yojna: 1. It is safe motherhood intervention of the…N6.9 · N6.2

Economy has three exact repeats: the money multiplier (2019 → 2021), the most inflationary way to finance a deficit (2013 → 2021) and inclusive growth under the 11th Plan (2009 → 2010).

15Elimination analysis

“How many” questions: keyed answerQ
